========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because… I don’t know, my thinking feels like it’s slowing down or getting stuck. Like my brain just stalls out.

At work I’ll have a test report open, or even something simple like a CAD note, and I’ll just sit there staring at it. It can be an hour, honestly, and I still can’t decide what the next sentence should be, or which checkbox to mark. It’s not even always complicated work, which makes it more frustrating, because I know I should be able to do it. I have the file open, I know what it’s for, but it’s like nothing connects properly in my head. I just sit there trying to force the next step and it doesn’t come.

Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it all at once, but when I think about it, the things I used to do because I actually liked them have kind of just… stopped feeling like anything.

Like, I have a little printer I use for miniatures, and there’s been this same half-finished figure sitting there for months. Normally I’d be tweaking the settings or sanding little pieces or painting details, and I could lose hours doing that in a good way. But now I see it sitting there and it just feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with. I don’t even feel curious about finishing it.

And even things that are usually easier, like yoga or going for a walk in the woods, those used to help me reset. Now they feel like chores. Like I’ll think, “I should go walk, it would probably be good for me,” but there’s no pull toward it. It’s more like adding another task to a list I already can’t get through.

I have the file open, I know what it’s for, but it’s like nothing connects properly in my head. I just sit there trying to force the next step and it doesn’t come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around crying all the time or anything obvious like that. It’s more like I feel kind of weighed down, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s “sad” exactly.\n\nI know something feels off, and I’ve been frustrated with myself a lot because I can’t seem to function the way I usually do. But if you ask, “Are you sad?” I kind of hesitate, because it doesn’t feel like a normal sadness with a clear reason. It’s more like everything feels heavy and hard to get through, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it all at once, but when I think about it, the things I used to do because I actually liked them have kind of just… stopped feeling like anything.\n\nLike, I have a little printer I use for miniatures, and there’s been this same half-finished figure sitting there for months. I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, like I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not enough that I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve clearly gained or lost weight.”

Not really… I mean, I know you’re asking about people specifically, but it’s sort of the same feeling I was trying to explain with everything else. It’s like the part of me that used to get pulled toward things just isn’t really working.

Like with my miniature printer, I have this half-finished figure that’s been sitting there for months, and normally I’d be excited to mess with it, adjust things, paint it, whatever. Now I just look at it and feel nothing except that it’s another thing I haven’t done.

And even yoga or walking in the woods, which used to feel calming or at least like something I wanted to do, now it’s like… I have to convince myself, and even then it feels like a task. So with social stuff, it’s not that I hate people or anything. It’s more like I don’t feel that enjoyment or pull toward things the way I used to.

Yes… I think so. It’s actually been noticeable enough that other people are reacting to it, not just me feeling like I’m dragging.

At work, in meetings, I’ll know what I’m trying to say, but it takes me so long to get the words out that people start finishing my sentences. Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. I’ll be sitting there trying to answer a pretty basic question, and there’s this delay, like my brain and my mouth aren’t lining up.

And physically, too… even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly effortful. It’s not far, it’s a normal walk I’ve done a million times, but lately it feels like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I just feel slowed down.

I mean, I’m sleeping, I think. It’s actually been noticeable enough that other people are reacting to it, not just me feeling like I’m dragging.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I’ll know what I’m trying to say, but it takes me so long to get the words out that people start finishing my sentences. Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. I’ll be sitting there trying to answer a pretty basic question, and there’s this delay, like my brain and my mouth aren’t lining up.\n\nAnd physically, too… even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly effortful. It’s not far, it’s a normal walk I’ve done a million times, but lately it feels like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been since I got moved into the prototype-testing role. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.

It didn’t feel like one clear day where everything changed, but when I try to trace it back, that’s the point where I remember starting to feel this way. At first I thought it was just adjusting to the new position, like maybe I was stressed or out of rhythm, and I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it. But it just sort of kept going.

So… roughly a year and a month. That’s the best marker I have.

description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: Even when my supervisor shows me the data and says the prototype is fine, I still know that calibration error exposed something serious and that people at work are quietly tracking my files to build a case against me.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: In the lab after hours, I sometimes hear someone behind the test bench say, 'You ruined it,' as clearly as a coworker speaking, but when I turn around there's no one there.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: My printer for miniatures has been sitting with the same half-finished figure for months, and even yoga or walking in the woods feels like another task instead of something I actually want to do.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: People have started finishing my sentences in meetings because it takes me so long to answer, and even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels like I'm moving through thick syrup.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying tiny mistakes from the lab and telling myself I'm not just bad at this job but bad as a person, like I deserve whatever consequences come from it.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: On my commute I catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn't arrive anywhere, and sometimes the idea of steering off the road scares me enough that I grip the wheel and pull over.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the major depressive episode\n  manifestation: I never had experiences like hearing voices in the lab or feeling certain people were building a case against me before this depression started after the role change. Outside work, I have mostly stopped seeing people or doing the routines that kept me steady.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since I was moved into the prototype-testing role about a year and a month ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 25 / engineering_technologist_or_technician / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions, like spending time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you’re asking about people specifically, but it’s sort of the same feeling I was trying to explain with everything else. It’s like the part of me that used to get pulled toward things just isn’t really working.\n\nLike with my miniature printer, I have this half-finished figure that’s been sitting there for months, and normally I’d be excited to mess with it, adjust things, paint it, whatever. Now I just look at it and feel nothing except that it’s another thing I haven’t done.\n\nAnd even yoga or walking in the woods, which used to feel calming or at least like something I wanted to do, now it’s like… I have to convince myself, and even then it feels like a task. So with social stuff, it’s not that I hate people or anything. It’s more like I don’t feel that enjoyment or pull toward things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s actually been noticeable enough that other people are reacting to it, not just me feeling like I’m dragging.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I’ll know what I’m trying to say, but it takes me so long to get the words out that people start finishing my sentences. Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. I’ll be sitting there trying to answer a pretty basic question, and there’s this delay, like my brain and my mouth aren’t lining up.\n\nAnd physically, too… even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly effortful. It’s not far, it’s a normal walk I’ve done a million times, but lately it feels like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I just feel slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been since I got moved into the prototype-testing role. At first I thought it was just adjusting to the new position, like maybe I was stressed or out of rhythm, and I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it. But it just sort of kept going.\n\nSo… roughly a year and a month. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… a lot. More than makes sense, I think.

It’s usually little things from the lab that get stuck in my head. Like if I entered something slightly wrong, or missed a step and had to correct it, or asked a question I feel like I should’ve already known the answer to. I’ll replay it over and over afterward, sometimes for hours, and it doesn’t stay as just, “I made a mistake.” It turns into this whole… proof, I guess, that I’m not just bad at the job but bad as a person.

And I know that probably sounds dramatic, because they’re not always huge mistakes. Sometimes they’re tiny things that other people probably forget five minutes later. But in my head it feels like this record of everything wrong with me. Like I deserve whatever happens because of it—if people are disappointed in me, or if I get in trouble, or if things get worse at work. It feels like I earned it somehow.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, yes, I’ve had thoughts like that. It’s hard to say out loud because part of me wants to immediately explain that I’m not trying to be dramatic, and I don’t want anyone to overreact, but… it’s been happening.

It’s mostly when I’m driving, on my commute. I’ll be going to work or coming home, and I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be easier if I just… didn’t arrive anywhere. Like if I could just stop having to get there, stop having to keep showing up. And then sometimes the thought gets more specific, like steering off the road, and that scares me. It’s not like I sit down and make a plan, but the fact that the idea even shows up in my head while I’m behind the wheel really frightens me.

There have been times I grip the wheel really hard because I’m scared of myself for even thinking it. A couple of times I’ve pulled over just to sit there until it passes, because I don’t trust how close the thought feels in that moment. I hate admitting that. It makes me feel ashamed and also kind of terrified, because I don’t want to hurt myself, but the thought is there.

But if you ask, “Are you sad?” I kind of hesitate, because it doesn’t feel like a normal sadness with a clear reason. It’s more like everything feels heavy and hard to get through, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it all at once, but when I think about it, the things I used to do because I actually liked them have kind of just… stopped feeling like anything.\n\nLike, I have a little printer I use for miniatures, and there’s been this same half-finished figure sitting there for months. Normally I’d be tweaking the settings or sanding little pieces or painting details, and I could lose hours doing that in a good way. But now I see it sitting there and it just feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with. I don’t even feel curious about finishing it.\n\nAnd even things that are usually easier, like yoga or going for a walk in the woods, those used to help me reset. Now they feel like chores. Like I’ll think, “I should go walk, it would probably be good for me,” but there’s no pull toward it. It’s more like adding another task to a list I already can’t get through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, like I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or anything. I’m not paying very good attention to that kind of thing lately. But nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that for sure. I mean, I’m sleeping, I think. I haven’t been keeping track of the hours or anything, and the days kind of blur together enough that it’s hard for me to say, like, “Yes, I slept six hours” or “No, I was up all night.”\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed some huge obvious change, like being awake until sunrise every night or sleeping through entire days. But I also don’t feel very clear about it. Sometimes I’m in bed and I’m not sure how much of that time was actually sleeping versus just lying there, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say confidently. I haven’t really been paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions, like spending time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you’re asking about people specifically, but it’s sort of the same feeling I was trying to explain with everything else. Now I just look at it and feel nothing except that it’s another thing I haven’t done.\n\nAnd even yoga or walking in the woods, which used to feel calming or at least like something I wanted to do, now it’s like… I have to convince myself, and even then it feels like a task. So with social stuff, it’s not that I hate people or anything. It’s more like I don’t feel that enjoyment or pull toward things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s actually been noticeable enough that other people are reacting to it, not just me feeling like I’m dragging.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I’ll know what I’m trying to say, but it takes me so long to get the words out that people start finishing my sentences. Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. I’ll be sitting there trying to answer a pretty basic question, and there’s this delay, like my brain and my mouth aren’t lining up.\n\nAnd physically, too… even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly effortful. It’s not far, it’s a normal walk I’ve done a million times, but lately it feels like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I just feel slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been since I got moved into the prototype-testing role. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nIt didn’t feel like one clear day where everything changed, but when I try to trace it back, that’s the point where I remember starting to feel this way. At first I thought it was just adjusting to the new position, like maybe I was stressed or out of rhythm, and I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it. But it just sort of kept going.\n\nSo… roughly a year and a month. That’s the best marker I have.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you've done wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… a lot. More than makes sense, I think.\n\nIt’s usually little things from the lab that get stuck in my head. Like if I entered something slightly wrong, or missed a step and had to correct it, or asked a question I feel like I should’ve already known the answer to. I’ll replay it over and over afterward, sometimes for hours, and it doesn’t stay as just, “I made a mistake.” It turns into this whole… proof, I guess, that I’m not just bad at the job but bad as a person.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds dramatic, because they’re not always huge mistakes. Sometimes they’re tiny things that other people probably forget five minutes later. But in my head it feels like this record of everything wrong with me. Like I deserve whatever happens because of it—if people are disappointed in me, or if I get in trouble, or if things get worse at work. It feels like I earned it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, I’ve had thoughts like that. It’s hard to say out loud because part of me wants to immediately explain that I’m not trying to be dramatic, and I don’t want anyone to overreact, but… it’s been happening.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m driving, on my commute. I’ll be going to work or coming home, and I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be easier if I just… didn’t arrive anywhere. Like if I could just stop having to get there, stop having to keep showing up. And then sometimes the thought gets more specific, like steering off the road, and that scares me. It’s not like I sit down and make a plan, but the fact that the idea even shows up in my head while I’m behind the wheel really frightens me.\n\nThere have been times I grip the wheel really hard because I’m scared of myself for even thinking it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… most of the day, honestly. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally want to do things has gone quiet.

I have this miniature printer at home, for little figures, and there’s been the same half-finished one sitting there for months. I keep seeing it and thinking I should finish it, because I used to really like doing that—tweaking the settings, cleaning up the details, painting them after. It used to feel satisfying. Now it just sits there, and even thinking about starting it again feels heavy, like it would take more from me than I have.

And it’s not just that. Yoga used to help me feel a little more in my body, and walking in the woods used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now even those feel like chores on a list. Like, “I should go for a walk because it’s good for me,” or “I should do yoga because people say it helps,” but there’s no real pull toward it. It’s not enjoyment so much as another task I’m failing to do.

It’s more like I’m slowed down, I think. I mean, I can feel restless in my head, like I’m uncomfortable being in my own skin sometimes, but physically it’s mostly this… lag. Like everything takes longer than it should.

At work it’s been embarrassing because in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences for me. Not in a mean way, necessarily, but because I take too long to answer. I’ll know there’s something I’m supposed to say, and I’m trying to get the words lined up, but there’s this pause that stretches out and I can see people waiting. Then someone jumps in with what they think I mean, and usually they’re close enough that I just nod because it feels too hard to correct it.

Even moving around feels slow. Walking from my desk to the test chamber feels like I’m pushing through thick syrup or something. It’s not that the distance is far, and it’s not like I’m injured. It just feels like my body is delayed, like every step takes effort and I have to consciously make myself keep going.

It’s more like adding another task to a list I already can’t get through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, like I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not enough that I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve clearly gained or lost weight.”\n\nI mean, I could have fluctuated a little and just not noticed. I’m not paying very good attention to that kind of thing lately. But nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that for sure. I mean, I’m sleeping, I think. I haven’t been keeping track of the hours or anything, and the days kind of blur together enough that it’s hard for me to say, like, “Yes, I slept six hours” or “No, I was up all night.”\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed some huge obvious change, like being awake until sunrise every night or sleeping through entire days. But I also don’t feel very clear about it. Sometimes I’m in bed and I’m not sure how much of that time was actually sleeping versus just lying there, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say confidently. I haven’t really been paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions, like spending time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you’re asking about people specifically, but it’s sort of the same feeling I was trying to explain with everything else. It’s like the part of me that used to get pulled toward things just isn’t really working.\n\nLike with my miniature printer, I have this half-finished figure that’s been sitting there for months, and normally I’d be excited to mess with it, adjust things, paint it, whatever. Now I just look at it and feel nothing except that it’s another thing I haven’t done.\n\nAnd even yoga or walking in the woods, which used to feel calming or at least like something I wanted to do, now it’s like… I have to convince myself, and even then it feels like a task. So with social stuff, it’s not that I hate people or anything. It’s more like I don’t feel that enjoyment or pull toward things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s actually been noticeable enough that other people are reacting to it, not just me feeling like I’m dragging.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I’ll know what I’m trying to say, but it takes me so long to get the words out that people start finishing my sentences. Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. I’ll be sitting there trying to answer a pretty basic question, and there’s this delay, like my brain and my mouth aren’t lining up.\n\nAnd physically, too… even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly effortful. It’s not far, it’s a normal walk I’ve done a million times, but lately it feels like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I just feel slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been since I got moved into the prototype-testing role. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nIt didn’t feel like one clear day where everything changed, but when I try to trace it back, that’s the point where I remember starting to feel this way. At first I thought it was just adjusting to the new position, like maybe I was stressed or out of rhythm, and I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it. But it just sort of kept going.\n\nSo… roughly a year and a month. That’s the best marker I have.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you've done wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… a lot. More than makes sense, I think.\n\nIt’s usually little things from the lab that get stuck in my head. Like if I entered something slightly wrong, or missed a step and had to correct it, or asked a question I feel like I should’ve already known the answer to. I’ll replay it over and over afterward, sometimes for hours, and it doesn’t stay as just, “I made a mistake.” It turns into this whole… proof, I guess, that I’m not just bad at the job but bad as a person.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds dramatic, because they’re not always huge mistakes. Sometimes they’re tiny things that other people probably forget five minutes later. But in my head it feels like this record of everything wrong with me. Like I deserve whatever happens because of it—if people are disappointed in me, or if I get in trouble, or if things get worse at work. It feels like I earned it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, I’ve had thoughts like that. It’s hard to say out loud because part of me wants to immediately explain that I’m not trying to be dramatic, and I don’t want anyone to overreact, but… it’s been happening.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m driving, on my commute. I’ll be going to work or coming home, and I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be easier if I just… didn’t arrive anywhere. Like if I could just stop having to get there, stop having to keep showing up. And then sometimes the thought gets more specific, like steering off the road, and that scares me. It’s not like I sit down and make a plan, but the fact that the idea even shows up in my head while I’m behind the wheel really frightens me.\n\nThere have been times I grip the wheel really hard because I’m scared of myself for even thinking it. A couple of times I’ve pulled over just to sit there until it passes, because I don’t trust how close the thought feels in that moment. I hate admitting that. It makes me feel ashamed and also kind of terrified, because I don’t want to hurt myself, but the thought is there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in almost all activities most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, honestly. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally want to do things has gone quiet.\n\nI have this miniature printer at home, for little figures, and there’s been the same half-finished one sitting there for months. I keep seeing it and thinking I should finish it, because I used to really like doing that—tweaking the settings, cleaning up the details, painting them after. It used to feel satisfying. Now it just sits there, and even thinking about starting it again feels heavy, like it would take more from me than I have.\n\nAnd it’s not just that. Yoga used to help me feel a little more in my body, and walking in the woods used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now even those feel like chores on a list. Like, “I should go for a walk because it’s good for me,” or “I should do yoga because people say it helps,” but there’s no real pull toward it. It’s not enjoyment so much as another task I’m failing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're restless or fidgety, or is it more that you feel slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m slowed down, I think. I mean, I can feel restless in my head, like I’m uncomfortable being in my own skin sometimes, but physically it’s mostly this… lag. Like everything takes longer than it should.\n\nAt work it’s been embarrassing because in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences for me. Not in a mean way, necessarily, but because I take too long to answer. I’ll know there’s something I’m supposed to say, and I’m trying to get the words lined up, but there’s this pause that stretches out and I can see people waiting. Then someone jumps in with what they think I mean, and usually they’re close enough that I just nod because it feels too hard to correct it.\n\nEven moving around feels slow. Walking from my desk to the test chamber feels like I’m pushing through thick syrup or something. It’s not that the distance is far, and it’s not like I’m injured. It just feels like my body is delayed, like every step takes effort and I have to consciously make myself keep going.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s hard to say out loud because part of me wants to immediately explain that I’m not trying to be dramatic, and I don’t want anyone to overreact, but… it’s been happening.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m driving, on my commute. I’ll be going to work or coming home, and I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be easier if I just… didn’t arrive anywhere. Like if I could just stop having to get there, stop having to keep showing up. And then sometimes the thought gets more specific, like steering off the road, and that scares me. It’s not like I sit down and make a plan, but the fact that the idea even shows up in my head while I’m behind the wheel really frightens me.\n\nThere have been times I grip the wheel really hard because I’m scared of myself for even thinking it. A couple of times I’ve pulled over just to sit there until it passes, because I don’t trust how close the thought feels in that moment. I hate admitting that. It makes me feel ashamed and also kind of terrified, because I don’t want to hurt myself, but the thought is there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in almost all activities most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, honestly. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally want to do things has gone quiet.\n\nI have this miniature printer at home, for little figures, and there’s been the same half-finished one sitting there for months. I keep seeing it and thinking I should finish it, because I used to really like doing that—tweaking the settings, cleaning up the details, painting them after. It used to feel satisfying. Now it just sits there, and even thinking about starting it again feels heavy, like it would take more from me than I have.\n\nAnd it’s not just that. Yoga used to help me feel a little more in my body, and walking in the woods used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now even those feel like chores on a list. Like, “I should go for a walk because it’s good for me,” or “I should do yoga because people say it helps,” but there’s no real pull toward it. It’s not enjoyment so much as another task I’m failing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're restless or fidgety, or is it more that you feel slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m slowed down, I think. I mean, I can feel restless in my head, like I’m uncomfortable being in my own skin sometimes, but physically it’s mostly this… lag. Like everything takes longer than it should.\n\nAt work it’s been embarrassing because in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences for me. Not in a mean way, necessarily, but because I take too long to answer. I’ll know there’s something I’m supposed to say, and I’m trying to get the words lined up, but there’s this pause that stretches out and I can see people waiting. Then someone jumps in with what they think I mean, and usually they’re close enough that I just nod because it feels too hard to correct it.\n\nEven moving around feels slow. Walking from my desk to the test chamber feels like I’m pushing through thick syrup or something. It’s not that the distance is far, and it’s not like I’m injured. It just feels like my body is delayed, like every step takes effort and I have to consciously make myself keep going.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're worse in the morning or if it's more consistent throughout the day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 25 / engineering_technologist_or_technician / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure. I’ve tried to figure that out, actually, because I keep wondering if there’s a pattern I’m missing, but it doesn’t feel clear-cut to me.

Mornings can be hard, but I don’t know if they’re hard in a different way or if I just notice it more because I have to start the day and immediately prove I can function. Sometimes waking up and realizing I have to go through everything again feels… heavy. But then later in the day, it doesn’t necessarily lift. It’s not like I can say, “Oh, by afternoon I’m better,” or “evenings are always worse.” It kind of just stays there, maybe shifting around a little, but not in a way I can confidently map out.

So I guess I’d say it’s more consistent throughout the day, but with mornings feeling especially noticeable sometimes just because starting feels so difficult. I don’t know if that answers it exactly.

I’m not paying very good attention to that kind of thing lately. But I also don’t feel very clear about it. Sometimes I’m in bed and I’m not sure how much of that time was actually sleeping versus just lying there, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say confidently. I haven’t really been paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions, like spending time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you’re asking about people specifically, but it’s sort of the same feeling I was trying to explain with everything else. It’s like the part of me that used to get pulled toward things just isn’t really working.\n\nLike with my miniature printer, I have this half-finished figure that’s been sitting there for months, and normally I’d be excited to mess with it, adjust things, paint it, whatever. Now I just look at it and feel nothing except that it’s another thing I haven’t done.\n\nAnd even yoga or walking in the woods, which used to feel calming or at least like something I wanted to do, now it’s like… I have to convince myself, and even then it feels like a task. So with social stuff, it’s not that I hate people or anything. It’s more like I don’t feel that enjoyment or pull toward things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s actually been noticeable enough that other people are reacting to it, not just me feeling like I’m dragging.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I’ll know what I’m trying to say, but it takes me so long to get the words out that people start finishing my sentences. Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. I’ll be sitting there trying to answer a pretty basic question, and there’s this delay, like my brain and my mouth aren’t lining up.\n\nAnd physically, too… even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly effortful. It’s not far, it’s a normal walk I’ve done a million times, but lately it feels like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I just feel slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been since I got moved into the prototype-testing role. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… most of the time, I think. It’s not that I can’t understand that something should be enjoyable, if that makes sense. Like, I remember enjoying it. I remember being the kind of person who would get absorbed in little projects.

I’ve got that miniature printer at home, and there’s this same half-finished figure that’s just been sitting there for months. I see it all the time, and part of me thinks, “You liked doing this. You should finish it.” But there’s no spark. No excitement about how it’ll turn out, no satisfaction in imagining painting it or fixing the details. It just feels like this object that’s quietly accusing me because I can’t make myself care enough to do it.

And even things that used to feel good in a simpler way, like yoga or walking in the woods, don’t really feel pleasurable now. I can still make a list in my head of why they’re supposed to help, but that’s all it is—a task. Like brushing my teeth or answering an email. I don’t feel drawn to them. I don’t get that sense of relief or enjoyment from them the way I used to. So, yes… I’d say I’m mostly not able to feel pleasure from things I used to actually want to do.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, honestly. It feels like my brain just… stalls out. Like it doesn’t move to the next step even when I know there is a next step.

At work I’ll have a test report open, or even something really simple like a CAD note, and I’ll just sit there staring at it. Not for five minutes—sometimes it’s like an hour has gone by and I still haven’t decided what the next sentence should be, or which checkbox I’m supposed to mark. And it’s not because the task is complicated enough to justify that much time. I know how to do these things. I’ve done them a lot. It’s more like I can see the work in front of me, but I can’t get my thoughts to line up into an action.

The decision-making part is bad too, because even tiny decisions start feeling weirdly impossible. I’ll think, “Okay, just write the note,” but then I get stuck on whether the wording is exact enough, or whether I’m missing something, and then I don’t choose anything. I just sit there, kind of frozen, and then I feel worse because I’m aware I’m wasting time, but knowing that doesn’t make me move any faster.

So yes… most of the time, concentration and decisions feel a lot harder than they should.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because… I don’t know, my thinking feels like it’s slowing down or getting stuck. Like my brain just stalls out.\n\nAt work I’ll have a test report open, or even something simple like a CAD note, and I’ll just sit there staring at it. It can be an hour, honestly, and I still can’t decide what the next sentence should be, or which checkbox to mark. It’s not even always complicated work, which makes it more frustrating, because I know I should be able to do it. I have the file open, I know what it’s for, but it’s like nothing connects properly in my head. I just sit there trying to force the next step and it doesn’t come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around crying all the time or anything obvious like that. It’s more like I feel kind of weighed down, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s “sad” exactly.\n\nI know something feels off, and I’ve been frustrated with myself a lot because I can’t seem to function the way I usually do. But if you ask, “Are you sad?” I kind of hesitate, because it doesn’t feel like a normal sadness with a clear reason. It’s more like everything feels heavy and hard to get through, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I didn’t notice it all at once, but when I think about it, the things I used to do because I actually liked them have kind of just… stopped feeling like anything.\n\nLike, I have a little printer I use for miniatures, and there’s been this same half-finished figure sitting there for months. Normally I’d be tweaking the settings or sanding little pieces or painting details, and I could lose hours doing that in a good way. But now I see it sitting there and it just feels like another thing I’m supposed to deal with. I don’t even feel curious about finishing it.\n\nAnd even things that are usually easier, like yoga or going for a walk in the woods, those used to help me reset. Now they feel like chores. Like I’ll think, “I should go walk, it would probably be good for me,” but there’s no pull toward it. It’s more like adding another task to a list I already can’t get through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, like I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not enough that I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve clearly gained or lost weight.”\n\nI mean, I could have fluctuated a little and just not noticed. I’m not paying very good attention to that kind of thing lately. But nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that for sure. I mean, I’m sleeping, I think. I haven’t been keeping track of the hours or anything, and the days kind of blur together enough that it’s hard for me to say, like, “Yes, I slept six hours” or “No, I was up all night.”\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed some huge obvious change, like being awake until sunrise every night or sleeping through entire days. But I also don’t feel very clear about it. Sometimes I’m in bed and I’m not sure how much of that time was actually sleeping versus just lying there, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say confidently. I haven’t really been paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions, like spending time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you’re asking about people specifically, but it’s sort of the same feeling I was trying to explain with everything else. It’s like the part of me that used to get pulled toward things just isn’t really working.\n\nLike with my miniature printer, I have this half-finished figure that’s been sitting there for months, and normally I’d be excited to mess with it, adjust things, paint it, whatever. Now I just look at it and feel nothing except that it’s another thing I haven’t done.\n\nAnd even yoga or walking in the woods, which used to feel calming or at least like something I wanted to do, now it’s like… I have to convince myself, and even then it feels like a task. So with social stuff, it’s not that I hate people or anything. It’s more like I don’t feel that enjoyment or pull toward things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s actually been noticeable enough that other people are reacting to it, not just me feeling like I’m dragging.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I’ll know what I’m trying to say, but it takes me so long to get the words out that people start finishing my sentences. Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. I’ll be sitting there trying to answer a pretty basic question, and there’s this delay, like my brain and my mouth aren’t lining up.\n\nAnd physically, too… even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly effortful. It’s not far, it’s a normal walk I’ve done a million times, but lately it feels like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I just feel slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been since I got moved into the prototype-testing role. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nIt didn’t feel like one clear day where everything changed, but when I try to trace it back, that’s the point where I remember starting to feel this way. At first I thought it was just adjusting to the new position, like maybe I was stressed or out of rhythm, and I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it. But it just sort of kept going.\n\nSo… roughly a year and a month. That’s the best marker I have.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you've done wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… a lot. More than makes sense, I think.\n\nIt’s usually little things from the lab that get stuck in my head. Like if I entered something slightly wrong, or missed a step and had to correct it, or asked a question I feel like I should’ve already known the answer to. I’ll replay it over and over afterward, sometimes for hours, and it doesn’t stay as just, “I made a mistake.” It turns into this whole… proof, I guess, that I’m not just bad at the job but bad as a person.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds dramatic, because they’re not always huge mistakes. Sometimes they’re tiny things that other people probably forget five minutes later. But in my head it feels like this record of everything wrong with me. Like I deserve whatever happens because of it—if people are disappointed in me, or if I get in trouble, or if things get worse at work. It feels like I earned it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, I’ve had thoughts like that. It’s hard to say out loud because part of me wants to immediately explain that I’m not trying to be dramatic, and I don’t want anyone to overreact, but… it’s been happening.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m driving, on my commute. I’ll be going to work or coming home, and I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be easier if I just… didn’t arrive anywhere. Like if I could just stop having to get there, stop having to keep showing up. And then sometimes the thought gets more specific, like steering off the road, and that scares me. It’s not like I sit down and make a plan, but the fact that the idea even shows up in my head while I’m behind the wheel really frightens me.\n\nThere have been times I grip the wheel really hard because I’m scared of myself for even thinking it. A couple of times I’ve pulled over just to sit there until it passes, because I don’t trust how close the thought feels in that moment. I hate admitting that. It makes me feel ashamed and also kind of terrified, because I don’t want to hurt myself, but the thought is there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in almost all activities most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, honestly. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally want to do things has gone quiet.\n\nI have this miniature printer at home, for little figures, and there’s been the same half-finished one sitting there for months. I keep seeing it and thinking I should finish it, because I used to really like doing that—tweaking the settings, cleaning up the details, painting them after. It used to feel satisfying. Now it just sits there, and even thinking about starting it again feels heavy, like it would take more from me than I have.\n\nAnd it’s not just that. Yoga used to help me feel a little more in my body, and walking in the woods used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now even those feel like chores on a list. Like, “I should go for a walk because it’s good for me,” or “I should do yoga because people say it helps,” but there’s no real pull toward it. It’s not enjoyment so much as another task I’m failing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're restless or fidgety, or is it more that you feel slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m slowed down, I think. I mean, I can feel restless in my head, like I’m uncomfortable being in my own skin sometimes, but physically it’s mostly this… lag. Like everything takes longer than it should.\n\nAt work it’s been embarrassing because in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences for me. Not in a mean way, necessarily, but because I take too long to answer. I’ll know there’s something I’m supposed to say, and I’m trying to get the words lined up, but there’s this pause that stretches out and I can see people waiting. Then someone jumps in with what they think I mean, and usually they’re close enough that I just nod because it feels too hard to correct it.\n\nEven moving around feels slow. Walking from my desk to the test chamber feels like I’m pushing through thick syrup or something. It’s not that the distance is far, and it’s not like I’m injured. It just feels like my body is delayed, like every step takes effort and I have to consciously make myself keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the morning or if it's more consistent throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I’ve tried to figure that out, actually, because I keep wondering if there’s a pattern I’m missing, but it doesn’t feel clear-cut to me.\n\nMornings can be hard, but I don’t know if they’re hard in a different way or if I just notice it more because I have to start the day and immediately prove I can function. Sometimes waking up and realizing I have to go through everything again feels… heavy. But then later in the day, it doesn’t necessarily lift. It’s not like I can say, “Oh, by afternoon I’m better,” or “evenings are always worse.” It kind of just stays there, maybe shifting around a little, but not in a way I can confidently map out.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s more consistent throughout the day, but with mornings feeling especially noticeable sometimes just because starting feels so difficult. I don’t know if that answers it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think. It’s not that I can’t understand that something should be enjoyable, if that makes sense. Like, I remember enjoying it. I remember being the kind of person who would get absorbed in little projects.\n\nI’ve got that miniature printer at home, and there’s this same half-finished figure that’s just been sitting there for months. I see it all the time, and part of me thinks, “You liked doing this. You should finish it.” But there’s no spark. No excitement about how it’ll turn out, no satisfaction in imagining painting it or fixing the details. It just feels like this object that’s quietly accusing me because I can’t make myself care enough to do it.\n\nAnd even things that used to feel good in a simpler way, like yoga or walking in the woods, don’t really feel pleasurable now. I can still make a list in my head of why they’re supposed to help, but that’s all it is—a task. Like brushing my teeth or answering an email. I don’t feel drawn to them. I don’t get that sense of relief or enjoyment from them the way I used to. So, yes… I’d say I’m mostly not able to feel pleasure from things I used to actually want to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to concentrate or make decisions most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, honestly. It feels like my brain just… stalls out. Like it doesn’t move to the next step even when I know there is a next step.\n\nAt work I’ll have a test report open, or even something really simple like a CAD note, and I’ll just sit there staring at it. Not for five minutes—sometimes it’s like an hour has gone by and I still haven’t decided what the next sentence should be, or which checkbox I’m supposed to mark. And it’s not because the task is complicated enough to justify that much time. I know how to do these things. I’ve done them a lot. It’s more like I can see the work in front of me, but I can’t get my thoughts to line up into an action.\n\nThe decision-making part is bad too, because even tiny decisions start feeling weirdly impossible. I’ll think, “Okay, just write the note,” but then I get stuck on whether the wording is exact enough, or whether I’m missing something, and then I don’t choose anything. I just sit there, kind of frozen, and then I feel worse because I’m aware I’m wasting time, but knowing that doesn’t make me move any faster.\n\nSo yes… most of the time, concentration and decisions feel a lot harder than they should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It just feels like my body is delayed, like every step takes effort and I have to consciously make myself keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the morning or if it's more consistent throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I’ve tried to figure that out, actually, because I keep wondering if there’s a pattern I’m missing, but it doesn’t feel clear-cut to me.\n\nMornings can be hard, but I don’t know if they’re hard in a different way or if I just notice it more because I have to start the day and immediately prove I can function. Sometimes waking up and realizing I have to go through everything again feels… heavy. But then later in the day, it doesn’t necessarily lift. It’s not like I can say, “Oh, by afternoon I’m better,” or “evenings are always worse.” It kind of just stays there, maybe shifting around a little, but not in a way I can confidently map out.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s more consistent throughout the day, but with mornings feeling especially noticeable sometimes just because starting feels so difficult. I don’t know if that answers it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think. It’s not that I can’t understand that something should be enjoyable, if that makes sense. Like, I remember enjoying it. I remember being the kind of person who would get absorbed in little projects.\n\nI’ve got that miniature printer at home, and there’s this same half-finished figure that’s just been sitting there for months. I see it all the time, and part of me thinks, “You liked doing this. You should finish it.” But there’s no spark. No excitement about how it’ll turn out, no satisfaction in imagining painting it or fixing the details. It just feels like this object that’s quietly accusing me because I can’t make myself care enough to do it.\n\nAnd even things that used to feel good in a simpler way, like yoga or walking in the woods, don’t really feel pleasurable now. I can still make a list in my head of why they’re supposed to help, but that’s all it is—a task. Like brushing my teeth or answering an email. I don’t feel drawn to them. I don’t get that sense of relief or enjoyment from them the way I used to. So, yes… I’d say I’m mostly not able to feel pleasure from things I used to actually want to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to concentrate or make decisions most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, honestly. It feels like my brain just… stalls out. Like it doesn’t move to the next step even when I know there is a next step.\n\nAt work I’ll have a test report open, or even something really simple like a CAD note, and I’ll just sit there staring at it. Not for five minutes—sometimes it’s like an hour has gone by and I still haven’t decided what the next sentence should be, or which checkbox I’m supposed to mark. It’s more like I can see the work in front of me, but I can’t get my thoughts to line up into an action.\n\nThe decision-making part is bad too, because even tiny decisions start feeling weirdly impossible. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not fidgeting, exactly. I don’t think I’m restless in the sense of tapping my foot or needing to get up all the time. It’s almost the opposite, honestly.

I feel slowed down. Like everything takes more effort than it should. Even at work, people have started finishing my sentences in meetings, which is… humiliating, honestly, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it takes too long to get it out. There’s this pause where I’m trying to pull the words together, and someone jumps in because I guess it seems like I’m done or stuck. And I am stuck, kind of, but not because I don’t care.

Even physically, walking from my desk to the test chamber can feel like I’m moving through thick syrup. Like my legs work, technically, but everything is heavy and delayed. So no, I wouldn’t describe it as restless. More like slowed down in a way other people are starting to notice.

Like I’ll think, “I should go walk, it would probably be good for me,” but there’s no pull toward it. It’s more like adding another task to a list I already can’t get through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, like I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not enough that I’ve thought, “Oh, I’ve clearly gained or lost weight.”\n\nI mean, I could have fluctuated a little and just not noticed. I’m not paying very good attention to that kind of thing lately. But nothing obvious comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that for sure. I mean, I’m sleeping, I think. I haven’t been keeping track of the hours or anything, and the days kind of blur together enough that it’s hard for me to say, like, “Yes, I slept six hours” or “No, I was up all night.”\n\nI guess I haven’t noticed some huge obvious change, like being awake until sunrise every night or sleeping through entire days. But I also don’t feel very clear about it. Sometimes I’m in bed and I’m not sure how much of that time was actually sleeping versus just lying there, if that makes sense.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t say confidently. I haven’t really been paying close attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions, like spending time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I know you’re asking about people specifically, but it’s sort of the same feeling I was trying to explain with everything else. It’s like the part of me that used to get pulled toward things just isn’t really working.\n\nLike with my miniature printer, I have this half-finished figure that’s been sitting there for months, and normally I’d be excited to mess with it, adjust things, paint it, whatever. Now I just look at it and feel nothing except that it’s another thing I haven’t done.\n\nAnd even yoga or walking in the woods, which used to feel calming or at least like something I wanted to do, now it’s like… I have to convince myself, and even then it feels like a task. So with social stuff, it’s not that I hate people or anything. It’s more like I don’t feel that enjoyment or pull toward things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s actually been noticeable enough that other people are reacting to it, not just me feeling like I’m dragging.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I’ll know what I’m trying to say, but it takes me so long to get the words out that people start finishing my sentences. Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. I’ll be sitting there trying to answer a pretty basic question, and there’s this delay, like my brain and my mouth aren’t lining up.\n\nAnd physically, too… even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly effortful. It’s not far, it’s a normal walk I’ve done a million times, but lately it feels like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I just feel slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been since I got moved into the prototype-testing role. That was about a year and a month ago now, I think.\n\nIt didn’t feel like one clear day where everything changed, but when I try to trace it back, that’s the point where I remember starting to feel this way. At first I thought it was just adjusting to the new position, like maybe I was stressed or out of rhythm, and I kept telling myself it would settle down once I got used to it. But it just sort of kept going.\n\nSo… roughly a year and a month. That’s the best marker I have.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you've done wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… a lot. More than makes sense, I think.\n\nIt’s usually little things from the lab that get stuck in my head. Like if I entered something slightly wrong, or missed a step and had to correct it, or asked a question I feel like I should’ve already known the answer to. I’ll replay it over and over afterward, sometimes for hours, and it doesn’t stay as just, “I made a mistake.” It turns into this whole… proof, I guess, that I’m not just bad at the job but bad as a person.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds dramatic, because they’re not always huge mistakes. Sometimes they’re tiny things that other people probably forget five minutes later. But in my head it feels like this record of everything wrong with me. Like I deserve whatever happens because of it—if people are disappointed in me, or if I get in trouble, or if things get worse at work. It feels like I earned it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, yes, I’ve had thoughts like that. It’s hard to say out loud because part of me wants to immediately explain that I’m not trying to be dramatic, and I don’t want anyone to overreact, but… it’s been happening.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m driving, on my commute. I’ll be going to work or coming home, and I’ll catch myself thinking that it would be easier if I just… didn’t arrive anywhere. Like if I could just stop having to get there, stop having to keep showing up. And then sometimes the thought gets more specific, like steering off the road, and that scares me. It’s not like I sit down and make a plan, but the fact that the idea even shows up in my head while I’m behind the wheel really frightens me.\n\nThere have been times I grip the wheel really hard because I’m scared of myself for even thinking it. A couple of times I’ve pulled over just to sit there until it passes, because I don’t trust how close the thought feels in that moment. I hate admitting that. It makes me feel ashamed and also kind of terrified, because I don’t want to hurt myself, but the thought is there.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in almost all activities most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the day, honestly. It’s like the part of me that used to naturally want to do things has gone quiet.\n\nI have this miniature printer at home, for little figures, and there’s been the same half-finished one sitting there for months. I keep seeing it and thinking I should finish it, because I used to really like doing that—tweaking the settings, cleaning up the details, painting them after. It used to feel satisfying. Now it just sits there, and even thinking about starting it again feels heavy, like it would take more from me than I have.\n\nAnd it’s not just that. Yoga used to help me feel a little more in my body, and walking in the woods used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now even those feel like chores on a list. Like, “I should go for a walk because it’s good for me,” or “I should do yoga because people say it helps,” but there’s no real pull toward it. It’s not enjoyment so much as another task I’m failing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're restless or fidgety, or is it more that you feel slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I’m slowed down, I think. I mean, I can feel restless in my head, like I’m uncomfortable being in my own skin sometimes, but physically it’s mostly this… lag. Like everything takes longer than it should.\n\nAt work it’s been embarrassing because in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences for me. Not in a mean way, necessarily, but because I take too long to answer. I’ll know there’s something I’m supposed to say, and I’m trying to get the words lined up, but there’s this pause that stretches out and I can see people waiting. Then someone jumps in with what they think I mean, and usually they’re close enough that I just nod because it feels too hard to correct it.\n\nEven moving around feels slow. Walking from my desk to the test chamber feels like I’m pushing through thick syrup or something. It’s not that the distance is far, and it’s not like I’m injured. It just feels like my body is delayed, like every step takes effort and I have to consciously make myself keep going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're worse in the morning or if it's more consistent throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I’ve tried to figure that out, actually, because I keep wondering if there’s a pattern I’m missing, but it doesn’t feel clear-cut to me.\n\nMornings can be hard, but I don’t know if they’re hard in a different way or if I just notice it more because I have to start the day and immediately prove I can function. Sometimes waking up and realizing I have to go through everything again feels… heavy. But then later in the day, it doesn’t necessarily lift. It’s not like I can say, “Oh, by afternoon I’m better,” or “evenings are always worse.” It kind of just stays there, maybe shifting around a little, but not in a way I can confidently map out.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it’s more consistent throughout the day, but with mornings feeling especially noticeable sometimes just because starting feels so difficult. I don’t know if that answers it exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think. It’s not that I can’t understand that something should be enjoyable, if that makes sense. Like, I remember enjoying it. I remember being the kind of person who would get absorbed in little projects.\n\nI’ve got that miniature printer at home, and there’s this same half-finished figure that’s just been sitting there for months. I see it all the time, and part of me thinks, “You liked doing this. You should finish it.” But there’s no spark. No excitement about how it’ll turn out, no satisfaction in imagining painting it or fixing the details. It just feels like this object that’s quietly accusing me because I can’t make myself care enough to do it.\n\nAnd even things that used to feel good in a simpler way, like yoga or walking in the woods, don’t really feel pleasurable now. I can still make a list in my head of why they’re supposed to help, but that’s all it is—a task. Like brushing my teeth or answering an email. I don’t feel drawn to them. I don’t get that sense of relief or enjoyment from them the way I used to. So, yes… I’d say I’m mostly not able to feel pleasure from things I used to actually want to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to concentrate or make decisions most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, honestly. It feels like my brain just… stalls out. Like it doesn’t move to the next step even when I know there is a next step.\n\nAt work I’ll have a test report open, or even something really simple like a CAD note, and I’ll just sit there staring at it. Not for five minutes—sometimes it’s like an hour has gone by and I still haven’t decided what the next sentence should be, or which checkbox I’m supposed to mark. And it’s not because the task is complicated enough to justify that much time. I know how to do these things. I’ve done them a lot. It’s more like I can see the work in front of me, but I can’t get my thoughts to line up into an action.\n\nThe decision-making part is bad too, because even tiny decisions start feeling weirdly impossible. I’ll think, “Okay, just write the note,” but then I get stuck on whether the wording is exact enough, or whether I’m missing something, and then I don’t choose anything. I just sit there, kind of frozen, and then I feel worse because I’m aware I’m wasting time, but knowing that doesn’t make me move any faster.\n\nSo yes… most of the time, concentration and decisions feel a lot harder than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or restless when sitting still?\n\nPatient: Not fidgeting, exactly. I don’t think I’m restless in the sense of tapping my foot or needing to get up all the time. It’s almost the opposite, honestly.\n\nI feel slowed down. Like everything takes more effort than it should. Even at work, people have started finishing my sentences in meetings, which is… humiliating, honestly, because I know what I’m trying to say, but it takes too long to get it out. There’s this pause where I’m trying to pull the words together, and someone jumps in because I guess it seems like I’m done or stuck. And I am stuck, kind of, but not because I don’t care.\n\nEven physically, walking from my desk to the test chamber can feel like I’m moving through thick syrup. Like my legs work, technically, but everything is heavy and delayed. So no, I wouldn’t describe it as restless. More like slowed down in a way other people are starting to notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I do. Probably more than I want to admit.

It’s like… I’ll make some tiny mistake at the lab, something that maybe another person would just correct and move on from, and I can’t let it go. I’ll replay it over and over in my head afterward, like I’m trying to prove something to myself. I’ll think about exactly what I said, what I should’ve checked, whether someone noticed, whether it means they all know I’m incompetent. And then it doesn’t stay as “I made a mistake.” It turns into “I am the mistake,” or… I don’t know, like I’m not just bad at the job, I’m bad as a person.

And I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, because these are sometimes really small things. Like a note entered wrong, or forgetting a step and having to go back, or asking a question I feel like I should already know the answer to. But in my head it becomes this whole moral failure. Like I deserve whatever consequences come from it. If I get in trouble, or people lose patience with me, there’s this part of me that thinks, “Well, of course. That’s what you get.”

So yes… I blame myself a lot. Not just for the thing itself, but for being the kind of person who would do it.

Not in a mean way, I don’t think—more like they’re trying to help or keep things moving—but it makes me feel really aware of how slow I must seem. Like every step takes more effort than it should, and I’m aware of how slow I’m going. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it’s not like I’m injured. I just feel slowed down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I guess… if I’m being honest, it’s been since I got moved into the prototype-testing role. Like if I entered something slightly wrong, or missed a step and had to correct it, or asked a question I feel like I should’ve already known the answer to. I’ll replay it over and over afterward, sometimes for hours, and it doesn’t stay as just, “I made a mistake.” It turns into this whole… proof, I guess, that I’m not just bad at the job but bad as a person.\n\nAnd I know that probably sounds dramatic, because they’re not always huge mistakes. Sometimes they’re tiny things that other people probably forget five minutes later. But in my head it feels like this record of everything wrong with me.
